# ScAI Handover

Architecture, module map, and developer guide for contributors. For end-user workflow, see the [User Guide](user-guide.md); for installation and the project overview, see the [README](../README.md).

**Version**: 0.36.13 · **Last updated**: 2026-07-30

## 1. Runtime Architecture

ScAI is a React/Vite frontend backed by FastAPI, LangGraph, and local Jupyter execution. REST handles configuration and lifecycle operations; WebSockets stream workflow and report progress. Long-running workflows are background asyncio tasks, active analysis state is cached in process, and durable session artifacts are written to disk.

The workflow implementation is split across mixins under `backend/workflow/`. `SingleCellAnalysisWorkflow` in `core.py` composes routing, context, messaging, callbacks, annotation, planning, agent nodes, synthesis, initialization, graph construction, reporting, and integration behavior. The LangGraph internal entry node is `restore_entry`; ordinary runs route from it to `initialize_round`. It is an implementation detail used to select the correct continuation path, not a user-facing workflow phase.

`WORKFLOW_GRAPH_NODES` in `backend/constants.py` is the authoritative registry for the 16 graph nodes: `restore_entry`, `initialize_round`, `pi_planning`, `planning_dialogue`, `user_review_gate`, `biochemistry_analysis`, `pathology_analysis`, `bioinformatics_analysis`, `literature_research`, `pi_synthesis`, `wait_for_user_input`, `check_convergence`, `integration_synthesis`, `generate_final_report`, `generate_graphic_abstract`, and `generate_detailed_report`. The principal route is planning, optional modality analyses, Bioinformatics Stage 1/2, literature grounding, PI synthesis, user feedback, and convergence routing. Conditional edges can return a plan for revision, begin another round, enter integration, or proceed to reporting. Planning failure can terminate directly at `END`; a blocked final report can also terminate before graphic-abstract and detailed-report generation.

## 2. Repository Layout

```
scai/
├── backend/            FastAPI app, agents, workflow engine
├── frontend/           React + Vite UI
├── clients/python/     Python SDK (scai_client)
├── docs/               User, administrator, and technical documentation
├── Dockerfile          Multi-stage build image
├── docker-entrypoint.sh Container startup
├── start.sh / stop.sh  Native launch / shutdown
├── build.sh            Build helper
├── requirements.txt    Python dependencies
└── LICENSE             Apache 2.0
```

## 3. Backend Module Map (`backend/`)

| Path | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| `main.py` | FastAPI app setup, middleware, lifespan tasks, and registration of 19 mounted routers. |
| `constants.py` | Application version, graph-node registry, node-to-agent mappings, workflow signals, and shared enums. |
| `workflow/` | LangGraph orchestration split into the 12 mixins composed by `core.py`; `graph.py` declares nodes and edges. |
| `agents/` | PI, Bioinformatics, Pathology, Biochemistry, Literature, Painter, Supervisor, and Technique implementations, with large agents split behind stable facades. |
| `routes/` | HTTP and WebSocket boundary modules; several large routers are split into sibling `*_parts/` packages. |
| `services/` | Session persistence, workflow task ownership, upload, cleanup, report generation, and other business services. |
| `models/` | Pydantic request, configuration, user, and analysis-state models; large schemas are split under `schema_parts/`. |
| `utils/` | LLM protocols, configuration resolution, Jupyter sandbox, authentication, storage, logging, and shared helpers. |
| `state.py` | Process-local session, workflow, task, lock, WebSocket, chat, report, and import-job registries. |
| `tests/` | Pytest suite, shared fixtures, structural guards, and pinned route registry. |

| `user_config.py`   | Per-user LLM endpoint, model, and credential configuration.                              |

## 5. Configuration and Agents

Agent settings resolve through `AgentSettingsProvider` in three layers: global `SystemConfig` defaults, per-user credentials resolved by `config_resolver.py`, and per-session overrides stored on `AnalysisState`. Agent code must use the provider rather than reading global configuration directly; live session changes are visible on the next access.

The core agents are PI, Bioinformatics, and Literature. A missing model for any core agent enters a user intervention gate. Pathology, Biochemistry, Painter, Supervisor, and Technique are optional and can be disabled when not needed. Most agents derive from `BaseAgent`; `SupervisorAgent` deliberately has a parallel interface and is not a subclass.

Bioinformatics execution uses Stage 1 for broad QC, clustering, and annotation, followed by Stage 2 downstream analysis. Later rounds pursue approved directions, and the integration phase correlates completed directions before reporting. HITL gates cover plan review, annotation confirmation, direction selection, round guidance, and recoverable agent failures.

| Path | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| `main.tsx`, `App.tsx` | React entry, error boundary, application shell, and top-level lifecycle. |

| `stores/appStore.ts`, `stores/` | Stable Zustand facade plus split domain state, persistence, migrations, and store composition. |
| `i18n/translations.ts`, `i18n/` | Stable translation facade plus English, Chinese, and prompt modules. |
| `pages/` | Authentication, setup, administration, and configuration pages. |
| `utils/`, `constants/`, `types.ts` | Shared transformations, browser storage, download helpers, constants, and TypeScript interfaces. |

## 7. Data & State

| Where                                   | What                                                                                                           |
|-----------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| `backend/state.py` (in-process)         | Process-local registries for sessions, workflows, analysis/chat/report tasks, WebSockets, exports/imports, locks, access times, and internal checkpoint jobs.              |
| Per-session disk directory | `session.json`, workspace files, uploads, state/checkpoint data, reports, and logs. |
| SQLite | Users/auth, encrypted user LLM configuration, and application metadata. |
| Frontend `localStorage` | Versioned Zustand UI persistence and JWT storage; server state is reloaded after migration. |

**Session lifecycle**: in-memory state is authoritative while active. `session_store.py` performs throttled disk writes and LRU eviction, while per-session locks serialize mutations. Portable export/import includes session data and generated artifacts; runtime workflow state remains managed by backend lifecycle services.

## 8. Snapshot V2 (in development — not stable)

> **Status: in development, disabled by default, not stable.** The feature is off
> unless an operator sets `SCAI_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2=true`, and the UI marks it
> experimental when it is on. On-disk layout, manifest schema and API shapes may
> change without a migration path, and the end-to-end behaviour has not been
> accepted on a real deployment — only under test, including real-kernel
> capture/restore acceptance. Treat stored snapshots as disposable: do not rely
> on one taken by an earlier build still restoring under a later one, and do not
> present the feature to end users. It is intentionally absent from the user
> guide.

Snapshot V2 captures a session's analysis state, notebook, workspace files,
LangGraph checkpoint and — when a kernel is live — the Jupyter runtime, so a
session can be returned to an earlier point of its analysis. It replaces the V1
checkpoint mechanism, whose restore path now answers HTTP 410; V1 records remain
listable and deletable for cleanup only.

### Storage model

Everything lives under the session directory, laid out by
`backend/services/snapshot_v2/paths.py`:

```
sessions/<id>/v2/          Snapshot V2 root
├── objects/               Content-addressed blobs, named by sha256
├── committed/             Published manifests (one JSON per snapshot), HMAC-signed
├── staging/               In-flight capture and restore working area
├── journals/              Transaction intent records (capture/restore/import/export/delete)
└── integrity/             Signature and evidence material
```

Objects are content-addressed, so an unchanged AnnData is stored once and shared
by every snapshot that references it — what grows the store is genuinely new
data, not the number of snapshots. A manifest names the objects a snapshot
consists of and carries a `root_hash` over its own canonical form; both the
manifest and the integrity material are HMAC-signed with the installation key.

### Lifecycle

| Operation | Entry point | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Capture | `SnapshotService.capture` (`service.py`, steps in `service_parts/capture_steps.py`) | Required captures at workflow boundaries are fail-closed; auto-captures are best-effort |
| Restore | `RestoreJobManager` → `RestoreCoordinator.apply` (`jobs.py`, `restore_parts/`) | Runs as a background job with a safe-point handshake; a pre_restore snapshot is taken first so the restore itself is undoable |
| Rollback | `recovery.py` `_RecoveryRestorer.rollback` | Reinstates the pre_restore image when a restore fails past its point of no return |
| Crash recovery | `recover_snapshot_transactions` (`recovery.py`), run at startup and on session load | Drives every non-terminal journal to a terminal state, or blocks the session |
| GC | `SnapshotGarbageCollector` (`gc.py`) | Mark-and-sweep over reachable objects. Runs on delete/import/recovery — **not** on a timer |
| Export / import | `exporter.py`, `importer.py` | Snapshots travel inside portable session archives; import defaults to `trust_runtime=false` |

### Concurrency and the safe point

Capture, restore, chat delegation and report generation can all be in flight
against one session, so mutating operations fence through a generation-scoped
cooperative safe point (`safe_point.py`). A capture or restore *requests* the
safe point; the workflow *acknowledges* it at a quiescent moment — a graph node
entry, a step boundary in the bioinformatics loop, or inside a HITL gate via the
gate's `safe_point_hook`. A busy owner surfaces as `SAFE_POINT_BUSY` → HTTP 409.
A restore that commits marks the superseded run, which unwinds with
`WorkflowSupersededError` rather than writing its stale position back.

### Continuation capsules

A snapshot records *where the workflow was*, not just its data. The graph's entry
node is `restore_entry`, and `build_restore_entry_plan` / `_route_from_restore_entry`
use the capsule to resume: `IdleCapsule` for a session with no live position,
`SemanticGateCapsule` for one parked at a resumable HITL gate, and a graph-node
capsule for a mid-round position. Do not assume a run always begins at
`initialize_round`.

### Capacity

Two ceilings, both checked before a capture writes and again after it lands: a
per-session store quota (`SCAI_SNAPSHOT_SESSION_QUOTA_GB`, default 500 GiB) and a
data-volume free-space reserve (`SCAI_SNAPSHOT_FREE_SPACE_RESERVE_GB`, default 50
GiB). The quota is per session, so the worst case is the quota times the number
of sessions holding snapshots; the reserve is the only volume-wide bound. The
snapshot panel advises a cleanup at 90% of the quota. See the
[Administrator Manual](admin-manual.md) for operator guidance.

### Known limits

- **Not stable.** Layout, schema and API may change without migration.
- **No object alias identity across restore.** Supported top-level values and
  workflow continuation are preserved, but the same AnnData embedded inside an
  arbitrary nested container is not guaranteed to be the *same object* after a
  restore. Code must not rely on identity checks such as `nested["adata"] is adata`.
- **GC is event-driven, not scheduled.** Space from superseded objects is
  reclaimed when snapshots are deleted.
- **No cross-session total.** Only the per-session quota and the volume reserve
  bound disk use.
- **Not accepted end to end on a real deployment.** Coverage is automated,
  including real-kernel capture/restore acceptance and a fault-barrier matrix,
  but no operator acceptance run has been recorded.

## 9. Request & Data Flow

1. **Config** — first-run dialog posts API keys to `/api/config`; backend validates and persists.
2. **Session create** — `POST /api/session/start` creates and persists the initial session state; `GET /api/sessions` lists sessions.
3. **Group + data upload** — biochemistry indicators via `/api/session/.../indicators`; files via chunked `/api/session/.../upload*`.
4. **Start analysis** — `POST /api/session/.../start` schedules the workflow task; WebSocket `/ws/{id}` streams agent messages, figures, status.
5. **HITL gates** — plan review, annotation confirmation, direction selection, and round feedback are submitted through interaction/lifecycle routes while the workflow waits.
6. **Report** — report routes launch generation and stream progress; final HTML and supporting artifacts are persisted under the session.
7. **Export / import** — tarball / zip created in the background, tracked as a job; import reverses the process.

## 10. Reports and Operations

Two detailed-report generators must remain aligned: `backend/agents/supervisor_agent.py` is the live generation path, while `backend/services/report/generator.py` is the fallback. Shared report services provide planning, sections, figures, rendering, scoring, variants, and regeneration. Exploration and integrated reports use the workflow/report variants alongside the detailed HTML report.

Docker and direct-run deployment are supported. For ports, volumes, environment variables, build flags, reverse proxy setup, user administration, backup, and troubleshooting, use the [Administrator Manual](admin-manual.md) or [管理员手册](admin-manual-zh.md) rather than duplicating operational parameters here.

## 11. Development Guide

- **Dev loop**: `./start.sh --dev` — hot reload for both frontend and backend.
- **Backend tests**: `pytest backend/tests`; `conftest.py` supplies isolated data and the route registry is pinned by `route_snapshot.json`.
- **Focused backend test**: `pytest backend/tests/test_specific.py::test_name`.
- **Frontend tests**: `cd frontend && npm test`.
- **Python client tests**: `pytest clients/python/tests`.
- **Adding a route**: create `backend/routes/<name>.py`, register it in `main.py`, update `route_snapshot.json` (`test_route_registry.py` will tell you if it drifts).
- **Adding an agent**: subclass `backend/agents/base.py` where appropriate, register settings, and wire the node through `backend/workflow/` and `backend/constants.py`.
- **Adding a workflow node**: update the relevant mixin, `workflow/graph.py`, `WORKFLOW_GRAPH_NODES`, node mappings, and workflow state.
- **Adding a report section**: update report services and keep both detailed-report generators in sync.
- **Conventions**: Pydantic models validate at route boundaries; services contain logic; agents are thin LLM orchestrators; `utils/` holds cross-cutting helpers; config is centralized in `backend/config/` with `PI_AGENT_NAME` as the single source of truth for the PI identifier.

## 12. Python Client SDK (`clients/python/`)

`scai_client` provides a `Client` and job-oriented batch API for submitting analysis configurations, waiting for completion, and downloading exports. Its package version is independent of the ScAI application version. See the [Python client README](../clients/python/README.md) for the authoritative API and examples.

## 13. Version History (condensed)

Detailed release notes for every version are available through `git log` and release tags. For versions before 0.30.1, changes are summarized by major version group; from 0.30.1 onward, each version is documented individually. The summary below highlights the architectural arc of the project.

**0.36.x — (2026-07-25 onward)**

- `0.36.13` — Snapshot V2 capacity and honest labelling: per-session quota raised to 500 GiB and the free-space reserve to 50 GiB, both made operator-tunable before startup; the panel advises a cleanup at 90% instead of letting the ceiling refuse a capture unannounced; and the subsystem is finally documented for developers and operators — as in development and not stable, still absent from the user guide, and marked experimental in the UI (2026-07-30):
  - **The two ceilings are operator-tunable (`backend/config/_config.py`, `docs/admin-manual.md`, `admin-manual-zh.md`)**: `snapshot_v2_session_quota_bytes` and `snapshot_v2_free_space_reserve_bytes` had defaults but no way to set them — no environment binding (unlike the `SCAI_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2` gate beside them) and no route exposing them, so `/api/config` reported only the boolean and an operator wanting different capacity had to edit code. New `SCAI_SNAPSHOT_SESSION_QUOTA_GB` and `SCAI_SNAPSHOT_FREE_SPACE_RESERVE_GB` are read once at import through `_parse_capacity_gb_env`, in GiB because that is the unit someone sizing a data volume works in while every check downstream is in bytes. An unset, non-integer or non-positive value logs and falls back rather than raising: this is import-time, so refusing would turn a typo in a deployment environment into a backend that cannot start. Defaults move from 100 GiB / 5 GiB to **500 GiB / 50 GiB**.
  - **A quota below the advised floor is warned about, not clamped (`backend/config/_config.py`)**: `warn_if_quota_below_advisory` logs at startup when the per-session quota is under `SNAPSHOT_SESSION_QUOTA_ADVISORY_MINIMUM_GB` (100 GiB) and returns whether it fired. The operator's value still applies — a small volume is a legitimate reason — but the symptom of an undersized quota is not gradual: a single runtime capture of a large AnnData already runs to tens of GB, and the store retains every distinct object across a session's lineage rather than only the newest, so the capture that crosses the ceiling fails closed and parks the analysis on a snapshot-failure gate. It is a callable rather than a bare `if` at module scope so it can be tested without reloading the module: a reload rebinds `default_config`, and every consumer that captured it by reference at import time keeps the old object, which leaks a different ceiling into the rest of the test session.
  - **The two modules that cannot import config no longer drift from it (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/object_store.py`, `runtime_parts/proof.py`)**: `SnapshotImportLimits` takes a duck-typed config and `proof.py` holds `RUNTIME_CAPTURE_FREE_SPACE_RESERVE_BYTES` at module scope, so both carry a hand-copied default — and a `SystemConfig` change that forgot them would leave the import path and the runtime-capture path enforcing the old ceiling while everything else moved. Both mirrors are updated and `test_capacity_defaults_mirror_system_config` now fails if they diverge again.
  - **The listing endpoint reports usage against the ceiling that will refuse the next capture (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/management.py`, `service_parts/queries.py`)**: `SnapshotStoreStatus` gains `quota_bytes`, `quota_usage_ratio` and `quota_warning`, resolved server-side so the fraction a user is advised at and the ceiling `_check_quota` enforces cannot disagree. The ratio compares *physical* bytes, matching what the quota counts — logical bytes count every reference and would warn far too early on a lineage that dedups well — and is deliberately not clamped at 1.0, since a store can legitimately sit above the ceiling after an operator lowers the quota or via the pre_restore grace allowance, and reporting 1.0 there would hide how far over it is. A non-positive quota yields no warning rather than a 100%-full store: that is "no ceiling known here", and advice the user cannot act on. `store_status()` passes the service's own resolved quota, because the constructor accepts a per-instance override and a status measured against the global default would advise a cleanup at the wrong point.
  - **The panel advises a cleanup at 90% (`frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`, `frontend/src/types/snapshotV2.ts`)**: an amber `role="alert"` block above the storage summary, in both languages, showing the percentage and both figures. It renders on the backend's `quota_warning` flag and never re-derives a threshold from the ratio, so the number the user sees and the number the capture enforces stay one value owned in one place. The quota trio is typed optional: a response without it — an older backend, or a fixture predating the fields — renders nothing rather than "NaN%".
  - **Snapshots are documented, and documented as experimental (`docs/HANDOVER.md`, `docs/admin-manual.md`, `admin-manual-zh.md`)**: the subsystem had no prose anywhere — only scattered route-table rows and changelog entries — so an operator had no way to learn what it stores, where, what it costs, or that it is not finished. HANDOVER gains section 8 (storage model, lifecycle table, the safe-point handshake, continuation capsules, capacity, and a known-limits list including the object-alias-identity residual); both administrator manuals gain section 8 (how to enable it, where snapshots live, event-driven GC, the per-session quota's worst case, transactional restore and recovery-blocking, what to monitor). Every new section opens with a status block: in development, not stable, disabled by default, layout and API may change without migration, no operator acceptance run recorded, and not a backup mechanism. Inserting a section shifted the numbering in all three files — the one prose cross-reference that moved (`section 12` → `13` for portable sessions) was updated, and the new sections' own references were corrected to the post-shift numbers.
  - **The user guide still says nothing (`docs/user-guide.md`, `user-guide-cn.md`)**: deliberately untouched, and verified to contain zero mentions. The feature is not offered to end users while it is experimental, so documenting it there would advertise a guarantee that does not exist.
  - **The snapshot panel says it is experimental (`frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`)**: an amber `Experimental` badge beside the title, with a hover explanation, and a one-line caveat strip under the header stating that the format may change and older snapshots are not guaranteed to restore. Both are unconditional rather than flag-driven: the panel is only reachable on an instance whose operator set `SCAI_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2`, so rendering at all means the feature is on. The caveat sits outside the loading branch so a slow listing cannot make it look like a settled feature. Three tests in `SnapshotQuotaWarning.test.tsx` pin the marking in both languages and before the listing resolves, so it cannot be dropped silently.
  - **New tests (`backend/tests/test_snapshot_v2_capacity.py`, `frontend/src/components/__tests__/SnapshotQuotaWarning.test.tsx`)**: 22 backend cases and 7 frontend cases covering the new defaults, the mirror guard, the parser's fallback behaviour on six unusable values, the advisory floor firing and not firing, the threshold's inclusive boundary at exactly 90%, an over-quota store, an unknown ceiling, physical-vs-logical comparison, a per-instance quota override, the panel's three render paths, and the experimental marking. Backend 3489 passed / 79 skipped, frontend 236 passed.

- `0.36.12` — Snapshot V2 edge-case hardening, in two audit passes over the whole subsystem (the second one auditing the first): the restore a user asks for at a HITL gate can be acknowledged at every one of the seven gates, quiescence is re-proved rather than assumed once, rollback is neither undone by the session cache nor half-reported, a crashed restore job or export stops protecting its target forever, object writes prove what they wrote and cannot leak their temp, the pre-write ceilings actually fire, pre_restore records a position that agrees with its own capsule, the discard-current escape hatch is both reachable and able to succeed, import/export atomicity and admission control, and the frontend effects that erased state or defeated their own bounds (2026-07-30):
  - **All seven HITL gates hand the safe point over (`backend/workflow/helpers.py`, `core.py`, `synthesis.py`, `annotation.py`, `planning.py`, `callbacks.py`)**: every HITL gate blocks *inside* a graph node, so the run task stays in flight and the graph never reaches another node entry — the only site that acknowledged the cooperative safe point. A restore requested while the user sat at a gate therefore had no acknowledger at all: `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` declines on the live run task and the graph is parked in `event.wait()`. It waited out its full 300s timeout and failed, while its pending request made every other snapshot operation answer `SAFE_POINT_BUSY` — and the gate is exactly where a user reaches for a restore. `wait_with_skip_time` now takes an optional `safe_point_hook` (the workflow's `await_restore_safe_point`), polled while blocked; time spent frozen is added back to the countdown so a restore hold cannot trip the auto-skip. Its five call sites cover the round, direction, integration, annotation, plan-review and species gates; the sixth gate is `_drive_hitl_gate` in `core.py`, the shared scaffold behind the LLM-failure, disabled-agent and marker-DB dialogs, which blocked on a bare `await event.wait()` with no hook *and no timeout* while `wait_with_skip_time` sat imported in the file and called nowhere in it. It now routes through the same helper, whose `wait_seconds=-1`-plus-hook branch preserves block-forever semantics exactly. Because the hook re-raises `WorkflowSupersededError` once a restore commits, that exception joins `asyncio.CancelledError` in the scaffold's cleanup branch so the gate does not leave `wait_input_reason` and its info attribute pinned on a state nobody will resume. A guard test fails if a gate is added without the hook.
  - **The headless runner is visible to the rest of the process (`backend/services/workflow_runner.py`)**: `start_workflow_if_needed` now registers its task in `_run_tasks` (and clears its own entry on exit). That dict is the process-wide proof that something is executing; the WebSocket runner was its only writer, so a v1-API run blocked at a HITL gate looked quiescent and a restore acknowledged its own safe point on behalf of a workflow that was very much alive and holding a live kernel — capturing pre_restore with `kernel_present: False` and tearing the checkpointer down under a running graph. The same handler now also clears the superseded flag, which every other `WorkflowSupersededError` site already did.
  - **Restore-job quiescence is re-proved, not proved once (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/jobs.py`)**: quiescence was established exactly once at enqueue (return value discarded), after which `_run_job` waited on nothing but `reservation.event`. That proof expires the moment the observed activity ends — and two of the five conditions `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` declines on, chat delegation and report generation, contain no acknowledgement site at all. A restore filed during either was orphaned deterministically: it burned its full 300s and reported `timed_out` on a session that had been idle for minutes, while its pending request made `_acquire_unlocked` refuse every other snapshot operation. The single `wait_for` is now a bounded poll against a monotonic deadline that re-runs `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` every `QUIESCENCE_RECHECK_SECONDS`; safe because the probe runs wholly under `state_lock` and the acknowledgement is generation-checked, so a run that registered meanwhile makes the probe decline and a workflow that acknowledged first leaves no pending request to match.
  - **A boundary capture that loses the safe point retries once (`backend/workflow/core.py`)**: the pending-request yield is a check-then-act whose only suspension point is the session lock a concurrent save holds across `to_thread`. A restore reserving the safe point in that window turned the required capture into a fail-closed gate that unwound the graph — so the workflow never reached another acknowledgement site and the restore that caused it waited out its own timeout. A *pending* request is a handover the workflow may complete, so it now does, once; an acquired foreign hold still fails closed.
  - **The post-report capture hands over a pending reservation too (`backend/workflow/reporting.py`)**: it was the one remaining required-snapshot producer that refused a merely *pending* safe point, and the report nodes contain no other acknowledgement site — so it is the last place a restore filed during report generation can be acknowledged. Both refusal routes ended the same way: without a live kernel the guard raised `LIVE_SANDBOX_REQUIRED` on `has_pending_request`, and on the live-kernel path the guard was skipped and the acquire below refused with `SAFE_POINT_BUSY`, since `_acquire_unlocked` cannot distinguish pending from acquired. Either armed the fail-closed gate on a run that had no snapshot problem, while the restore lost its only acknowledger. It now mirrors the boundary-capture handover, computing `kernel_present` *after* the freeze because a committed restore may have torn the sandbox down. An already-acquired foreign hold still fails closed — it never becomes pending.
  - **A parked session freezes its gate, but not a mid-node intervention (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/continuation.py`)**: `freeze_dynamic` short-circuited to `IdleCapsule` on `inactive: True`, and the quiescent-coordinator descriptor hard-codes that for every non-terminal status — including the `waiting*` statuses whose whole point is that the session is parked somewhere resumable. The status-derived gate lookup added in 0.36.8 was therefore unreachable for the producer it was written for: `pre_restore`, the snapshot a user reaches for to undo a restore, recorded no position at all. `inactive` still suppresses the mid-node `next_node` branch, where it genuinely contradicts a live graph position. Removing that short-circuit exposes a second question the status alone cannot answer: `waiting_input` also covers the `_drive_hitl_gate` interventions, and mapping those to `round_wait` froze a `SemanticGateCapsule` for a position the session was never at — restoring it set `wait_input_reason` to `round_wait` through the dispatcher's `or staged.gate` fallback while `agent_disabled_info` survived, so the reconnect resend no longer recognised the state and `/resume` — `round_wait` being a `SEMANTIC_WAIT_GATE` — answered `restored_wait_continuation` and drove the graph forward. `_gate_from_status` now returns None for `llm_failure`, `agent_disabled`, `marker_db_missing` and `snapshot_failure`, falling back to `IdleCapsule`: the honest answer, since the true position is mid-node and recovering it needs the checkpointer's `next`. The round gate, direction selection and integration selection are untouched — they all block in `_wait_for_user_input`.
  - **A restored gate lands on the status its own endpoint accepts (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/dispatcher.py`)**: the gate→status map is now a table. `plan_review` is a real capsule gate (`continuation.py` maps `waiting_plan_review` to it, and `freeze_dynamic` reaches that for `post_chat`/`pre_restore`), but every gate except `annotation_confirmation` was parked on `WAITING_INPUT`. `live_verify` only compares the gate, so the restore committed cleanly and left a session `POST /plan-review` rejects — with the workflow already evicted, its no-workflow branch, gated on exactly the status that was not written, was the only path left.
  - **A reused runtime does not drag the previous snapshot's position with it (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/pre_restore.py`)**: `capture_locked`'s runtime-reuse block re-pointed `analysis_state_ref`, `notebook_ref`, `checkpoint_ref` and `round_number` at the lineage head while the capsule built a few lines earlier was frozen from the LIVE session. The revision gate proves only that the kernel is unchanged, so this was two defects in one: unconditionally, the pre_restore snapshot re-declared the head's state, notebook and checkpoint, and a rollback to it silently discarded everything since that head which had not dirtied the kernel; and once the live round had advanced past the head, `manifest.round_number` was N while the capsule payload carried N+1, so `apply()` installed the round-N `session.json`, `activate()` overwrote `current_round` from the capsule, `live_verify` raised a `current_round` mismatch → `LIVE_VERIFY_FAILED`, and the session was left recovery-blocked. The reuse is now scoped to what the gate actually proves — `req.runtime = prev.runtime` plus `sandbox = None` — and the state/notebook/checkpoint are serialized fresh, which is close to free in a content-addressed store.
  - **Rollback is not silently half-undone, and reports the write it did make (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/recovery.py`, `backend/services/session_store.py`)**: `_persist_restored_state` installs the target state into the `sessions` cache before the transaction is verified, and recovery never touches that cache. So after a rollback rewrote `session.json` from the pre_restore snapshot, `ensure_session_loaded` returned the still-cached *target* object, cleared its recovery block and persisted it straight back over the file recovery had just restored — leaving an unblocked session whose state, files, notebook and checkpoint no longer agree. `RecoveryResult` now reports `session_state_rewritten` and the cache entry is evicted. That flag must also be reported when the rollback fails *after* its durable write: `rollback` streams the pre_restore state over the live `session.json` with the notebook and checkpoint steps still ahead of it, and when one of those failed the flag stayed False, the caller kept the cached target state, and `mark_recovery_blocked` wrote it straight back over the file the rollback had just restored — the same corruption by a different route. The restorer carries a `session_json_rewritten` flag, reset per transaction so it cannot leak across a scan, and both `except` handlers read it back. An extra eviction on a failure path is free; the next read comes from disk.
  - **A deferred recovery pass does not clear the block on the disk-load path (`backend/services/session_store.py`)**: of `ensure_session_loaded`'s three branches only the disk-load one omitted the `recovery_evaluated` term. Every lock-first endpoint (`/resume`, `/pause`, `/stop`, prompts, the interaction gates) takes the session lock before it loads, so `_snapshot_transaction_in_flight` — a bare probe of that same shared lock — always defers recovery for it; and once the cache-TTL sweeper has evicted a recovery-blocked session (it force-saves `recovery_blocked: True` and pops the cache but never touches `_recovery_blocked_sessions`), the deferred pass lands on exactly that branch, as does the eviction the rollback fix above performs. `clear_recovery_blocked` then discarded the process-wide marker, cleared the flag, stripped `recovery_error` and persisted the unblocked state, so `assert_recovery_clear` waved through a session whose `restore_apply` journal was still APPLYING and `/resume` built a workflow and started a kernel on a half-applied restore.
  - **Rollback quarantines a residual it never owned (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/recovery.py`, `backend/utils/runtime_apply.py`)**: with no `residual_ref` on the pre_restore manifest — the normal shape for a session with no live kernel, i.e. what users restore from — rollback skipped the residual block entirely and left the TARGET's pkl installed by `apply()`, with a valid signature. New `quarantine_live_runtime_pkl` mirrors the forward path's else-branch.
  - **`apply()` and rollback agree on who owns the checkpoint WAL (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/restore_parts/coordinator.py`, `recovery.py`)**: `apply` replaced only `checkpoints.sqlite`. Closing the checkpointer connection is what normally retires `-wal`/`-shm`, but that lives in `_retire_superseded_workflow`, which returns immediately when the session has no workflow object — the normal state after a restart, and one `_acknowledge_if_quiescent` supports — so orphans from an unclean shutdown were replayed over the restored image. Simply unlinking them is not the answer either: capture stores the checkpoint main file only, so commits still sitting in that WAL were never in any snapshot, and the WAL belongs to the very main file a rollback puts back — destroying it meant a restore that failed at a post-apply fence rolled back with those frames gone, while `rollback` returned True, `integrity_check` passed, nothing surfaced, and the session resumed at an earlier graph node. `apply` now moves the companions into `staging/<tx>/prev-checkpoints.sqlite-{wal,shm}` (falling back to an unlink if the move fails, since leaving them live is the corruption this addresses) and rollback reinstates them after restoring the main file. `forward_complete` never reads the stash — the pre-restore WAL must not be replayed over the target image.
  - **A crashed restore job stops protecting its target (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/recovery.py`)**: `_recover_restore_job_envelope` left every non-terminal `restore_job` journal in place, but `_run_job` is its only writer, so after a restart nothing could ever drive it terminal. `compute_protected_snapshot_ids` treats it as a live `restore_target`, so the snapshot became undeletable and un-reclaimable for good, status polls read `waiting_safe_point` forever, and `cancel` answered 404. A journal with no live handle is now marked `failed` with `RESTORE_JOB_ABANDONED`.
  - **A crashed export stops protecting its snapshots (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/exporter.py`, `recovery.py`)**: the same shape one journal kind over. `export()`'s own `finally` is the only remover and it runs in the process that wrote it, so a crash during the archive build — minutes over tens of GB, where a crash is most likely — left an ACTIVE journal with nothing to retire it, and `compute_protected_snapshot_ids` folds every `export_protection` envelope into the protected map: each snapshot the payload named answered 409 PROTECTED to DELETE and its bytes stayed unreclaimable by GC for the life of the session. "They only block deletes until an operator removes them" had no operator-facing removal path. Neither status nor age can tell debris from a live export — the journal reads ACTIVE for both, and the archive is built in a worker thread while recovery runs on the event loop, so recovery can legitimately land mid-export. The owner can: `_write_export_protection` now stamps `JOURNAL_OWNER_ID` as the import journal does, and `_recover_export_protection_envelope` reaps any journal carrying another process's id or none at all.
  - **The superseded flag is only set when something can clear it, and a superseded report run does not overwrite the restore (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/service.py`, `backend/workflow/core.py`, `backend/services/workflow_runner.py`)**: `_retire_superseded_workflow` marked the process-wide flag whenever a workflow *object* existed, but the object outlives its run and quiescent restores are admitted with nothing executing. Nothing then raises or catches `WorkflowSupersededError`, so the flag survived for the life of the process and a later, unrelated run unwound mid-round at its next node entry. The report paths get the same treatment from both ends. On the WebSocket path the flag *is* set and `generate_report`'s checkpointer-load `try` swallowed the exception from the node-entry hook, mislabelled it "Could not load state from checkpointer" and kept generating — producing reports and a post-report snapshot from the state the restore had discarded — while its trailing `except Exception` wrote FAILED, which `helpers.py` explicitly forbids for this exception; both handlers now delegate to `_superseded_report_result`, which also calls `clear_superseded()`, since no report path ever did. On the headless path the workflow is never registered in `workflows`, so `_retire_superseded_workflow` returns at its `workflow is None` guard and the flag is never set at all; `_build_workflow_for_session`'s `state_callback` had no is-current guard and wrote the pre-restore `AnalysisState` straight over the cache entry and `session.json` the restore had installed. That callback now compares object identity under `state_lock` — the headless counterpart of the WebSocket runner's `_write_state_if_current`, by identity because a committed restore installs a *new* state object and this workflow has no `workflows` entry to anchor on.
  - **The report runner closes the workflow it builds (`backend/services/workflow_runner.py`)**: `_run_report_generation` built a workflow and drove `generate_report`, which calls `initialize()` and therefore opens an aiosqlite WAL connection to the checkpoint database plus an `LLMManager`, a settings provider and the eager clients, and returned without `close()` — unlike the sibling `_headless_workflow_runner` whose `finally` does exactly that. The leak is bounded by cyclic GC, but the race is not incidental: the workflow is never in `workflows`, so `_retire_superseded_workflow` cannot reach it, and the orphaned connection can checkpoint stale WAL frames into a restored file.
  - **The three object writers are durable, proven and leak-free (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/object_store.py`)**: all three `put_*` writers fsynced the temp file and then `os.replace`d into `objects/` without fsyncing the directory, while `publish_staging` does fsync `committed/` — a power loss could leave a fully valid, HMAC-verified manifest whose objects were never linked, invisible to `verify_all_committed` (which checks signatures rather than existence), so the snapshot listed as healthy and only failed at restore. Cleanup was equally uneven: `put_file` had no `try`/`finally` at all and `put_stream`'s covered only its write loop, leaving `os.replace` guarded by nothing, so ENOSPC — the likeliest failure — stranded a near-complete multi-GB partial that nothing reclaims (recovery's staging sweep and `reconcile()` both skip dot-prefixed names by deliberate contract, quota accounting sums only `objects/`), and every HITL Retry leaked another copy. Both now have the same shape: one `try`, one `finally` that unlinks, a no-op once the rename has succeeded. `reconcile()` skips dot-prefixed entries too, so it is safe on its own terms rather than on its callers'. And `put_file` — the path for every h5ad, the dill residual, the checkpoint DB, the notebook and every workspace file — hashed the source in one pass and copied it in a second, unverified pass, making it the only writer with no post-write proof: a writer landing between the two passes got its bytes installed under the earlier hash, in a manifest that then commits and is signed clean, surfacing only at the restore rehash when the snapshot is already permanently unrestorable. It now accumulates a digest and byte count over the chunks it is already copying and refuses to install on a mismatch — no extra I/O.
  - **Both quota ceilings can actually fire (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/service_parts/capture_support.py`)**: the post-write gate `_check_quota` runs at capture step 8, after every object is on disk, yet subtracted `physical_added_bytes` from a `free` that already excluded them — demanding roughly twice the headroom the capture needed and failing deterministically on exactly the multi-GB AnnData captures this platform exists for, with Retry reproducing the same arithmetic forever. The pre-write estimate, for its part, summed only state, notebook, checkpoint and workspace — a few MB against the tens of GB a runtime capture writes — so the pre-I/O ceiling could essentially never fire for a runtime-bearing capture. It now projects the lineage head's runtime sizes, deduped against a local `seen` set only: deduping them against the store zeroes the projection for exactly the population it was written for, because GC marks every object of a verified committed manifest reachable and the head's own runtime objects are therefore present in `objects/` by construction. The head's refs model the bytes the next capture will write; they are not those bytes. Genuine reuse is short-circuited upstream, where `request.runtime is not None` returns 0 at the top.
  - **GC protects the audit bundle it was told to retain (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/gc.py`)**: the `audit-` prefix is a listing rule, but `mark()` inherited it as a reachability rule. On the `discard_current` restore path no pre_restore snapshot is taken, so the audit manifest is the only record of the state the user discarded — and the next sweep unlinked every one of its objects, leaving a manifest of dangling refs that no API can delete.
  - **The discard-current escape hatch is reachable *and* can succeed (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/service.py`, `pre_restore.py`, `frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`)**: the client has always branched on `PRE_RESTORE_UNAVAILABLE` to offer "discard my current state and restore anyway", and no producer ever emitted it — so that branch, `DiscardCurrentDialog` and the whole audit-bundle path behind it were dead code, and a user whose kernel could not be captured could only Retry into the same failure. Every pre-restore capture failure now reports that code (cause preserved in details), and the panel acts on it when it arrives on the transaction rather than only on the initial 202. Making the hatch reachable exposes that it could not work: `AuditBundle.capture_locked` handed the live sandbox to `_capture_locked_for_restore` with no `runtime` on the request, so `_capture_impl` re-drove the identical live-kernel capture whose failure produced the code in the first place. Every shared failure cause reproduced exactly, the bundle raised `AUDIT_CAPTURE_FAILED`, and `restore_snapshot` returned `AUDIT_FAILED` — a code the panel offers no action for. The capture is now factored into `AuditBundle._capture` and the kernel captured opportunistically: the live attempt stays first, since while it succeeds it is the only record of the discarded kernel, and on failure the bundle retries once with `sandbox=None` and an explicit `empty_complete_runtime()` — explicit because the empty-runtime eligibility gate refuses `reason="audit_bundle"` and would merely trade one terminal code for another. Non-kernel failures (quota, workspace, state) recur on the retry and still raise, so a bundle is never published on a guess; one published without the kernel records `AUDIT_RUNTIME_NOT_CAPTURED` carrying the original code.
  - **A live-kernel capture cannot publish an empty "complete" runtime (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/runtime_parts/capture.py`)**: the known-unpersistable pre-skip dropped a value that IS live in kernel globals without recording it in `dropped_present`, so the post-pruning fail-closed guard could not fire. The absent-name soft skip is deliberately left alone.
  - **The fail-closed runtime tier is reachable (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/runtime_parts/capture.py`, `service_parts/capture_steps.py`)**: `checkpoint_dependencies` has no production writer, so `mandatory_names` was always empty and the residual tier was "best effort, silently drop". `_collect_checkpoint_dependencies` now also reads the safe-point descriptor (the channel a boundary producer actually has, and the one `_resolve_kernel_present` already uses), and a canonical artifact that is LIVE in globals and that dill unexpectedly failed on now fails closed. Absent names and inherently unpersistable types keep their deliberate soft skip.
  - **A capture never spans two kernel generations (`backend/utils/sandbox.py`, `sandbox_parts/kernel.py`, `services/snapshot_v2/runtime_parts/capture.py`)**: new monotonic `kernel_epoch`, bumped by start and restart and asserted around every capture cell. `_execute_unlocked`'s pre-execute liveness guard restarts a dead kernel and then runs the caller's code on the new one, which `_restart_kernel` rehydrates from the last saved pkl — an older generation than the one the capture already partially serialized. A kernel reaped between two cells produced a manifest whose variables never coexisted, and every object hash in it is internally consistent, so nothing downstream could detect it.
  - **The capture templates unbind their own temporaries (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/runtime_parts/kernel_templates.py`)**: the templates run at the kernel's `__main__` scope, so every name they bind is a kernel global for the life of the kernel, and neither unbound anything. `_SAVE_ANNDATA_TEMPLATE` left `_to_write` — a full sanitized copy of the captured AnnData — plus seven references into it; because `_adata.copy()` is evaluated before the rebind, every later capture peaked at 3x the dataset where sanitize-then-write needs 2x. The waste was invisible: `save_session` skips `_`-prefixed names so it was never persisted, no probe measures kernel RSS, and only a kernel restart reclaimed it. `_SAVE_DILL_TEMPLATE` left `_verified_obj` the same way. Both now end in a `finally` that pops their own temporaries — a `finally` because a failed write is exactly when the host retries and builds another copy, with IPython keeping the failing frames alive on top of that. The cleanup uses only tuple literals so the stray-replacement-field guard on these `.format()` strings still holds, and sits after the markers the host parses.
  - **The live pkl wins over a stale h5ad sidecar (`backend/utils/sandbox.py`)**: the sidecar set is written only by restore/recovery and never retired, while `save_session()` dumps every live AnnData into the pkl. The restore template applied the dill frames and then rebound unconditionally from the sidecars, so every later rehydrate (kernel OOM, session reopen) rolled the namespace back to the restored snapshot — hashes still matched, so nothing flagged it.
  - **`atomic_replace_*` cannot leak a dataset-sized temp (`backend/utils/runtime_apply.py`)**: both functions copied into `<dst>.<pid>.staged` with no guard, so an exception between `copy2` and `os.replace` — ENOSPC being realistic, since restore holds the object-store copy, the staged copy and the live copy at once — left a dataset-sized partial in `<session>/states/`, a directory no sweeper covers: the states sweeps glob `*.h5ad`, GC walks `objects/`, and the snapshot staging sweeps operate on `<session>/staging`. It is not attributed to the session's quota and is packed verbatim into every v1 export. Both now unlink on `BaseException` before re-raising. Since the temp name is keyed on the pid that covers the in-process case completely, but a killed process cannot clean up after itself — so `_quarantine_unreferenced_live_h5ads` additionally reclaims `*.<pid>.staged` entries whose writing process is gone. Liveness, not the name, decides: a temp belonging to a running pid is an in-flight copy and unlinking it would sabotage a live restore.
  - **The post-publish reconciliation flag has a consumer (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/service.py`)**: `_capture_impl` deliberately does not raise when the session save after a successful publish fails, signalling `session_reconciliation_pending` instead — and nothing read it, so the escape hatch had no other end and the `saved_runtime_revision` written just before was simply lost.
  - **Snapshot-failure actions are durable and reachable, and every forward-motion endpoint refuses an armed gate (`backend/routes/lifecycle.py`, `backend/routes/interaction.py`, `backend/services/snapshot_v2/failure_gate.py`, `backend/services/snapshot_v2/errors.py`)**: Skip now closes the held HITL WebSocket the way Retry does, so the graph is actually re-driven instead of the session sitting at `RUNNING` with no workflow; Terminate persists STOPPED *before* the irreversible teardown; all three writes are forced, since RUNNING/PAUSED/STOPPED do not bypass the 5s throttle and the gate write they supersede was force-saved moments earlier. A bare `post_chat` gate persisted by a crash between the service-level write and the typed one can now be upgraded instead of leaving Terminate as the only exit. `SNAPSHOT_NOT_FOUND` maps to 404 regardless of producer class, so an idempotently-retried DELETE is no longer told the store is full. Three endpoints in `interaction.py` could release the fail-closed gate and now refuse with 409: `/human-input`, which signals the same `_continue_event` the round gate waits on; `/supplementary-analysis`, which signals it too through `signal_supplementary_analysis(target='direction'|'round')` and drove a full PI-planned supplementary run — bioinformatics agent, up to 30 kernel steps, workspace and AnnData mutation, report regeneration — past a boundary the design guarantees is snapshotted, so a later successful Retry would capture that work under the label of the boundary it was meant to precede (its guard is placed *before* the target/status branches, since `target='round'` has no status check at all); and `/finalize-from-integration`, which guarded only on `analysis_phase == INTEGRATION and status == WAITING_INPUT` — precisely the two conditions a required-snapshot gate leaves intact, as it parks on `WAITING_INPUT` and never touches `analysis_phase` — and ran the graph to COMPLETED with `snapshot_failure` still armed, no node, callback or `finally` between the endpoint and the report rechecking it.
  - **A terminal restore keeps the reports it is restoring (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/dispatcher.py`)**: `activate` invalidated all four report paths for every capsule kind "so a restore triggers fresh generation", but a terminal restore sets COMPLETED and both `run()` and `resume()` short-circuit before entering the graph — nothing can regenerate them. Restoring the one snapshot that represents the finished analysis left every report view answering 404, with the destructive re-analysis path as the only way back.
  - **Listing reconciliation is additive over V1 records (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/service_parts/queries.py`, `backend/services/session_store.py`)**: `rebuild_snapshots_from_manifests` built its listing from `verify_all_committed()` alone and returned it as the whole of `AnalysisState.snapshots`, so every legacy V1 record — `format_version` null/1, no committed manifest by construction — was dropped the first time `ensure_session_loaded` reconciled, and the next save persisted the loss. The V1 compatibility surface the module contract promises then went dead: `build_legacy_inventory` reported 0 and the legacy DELETE answered 404, leaving sidecars under `sessions/<id>/snapshots/` (which V2 GC never sweeps) reclaimable only by deleting the whole session. The reconcile now carries forward exactly the entries `legacy._is_v1_snapshot` accepts and never a `format_version >= 2` record, so a V2 projection whose manifest is gone still disappears. The baseline comes from a new optional `existing` argument, passed from the load path — the only place the V1 records can be read from — and defaulting to the cached listing so the capture/delete call sites stop re-dropping what the load path kept. The sort key was made tz-tolerant at the same time: V2 projections are aware, persisted V1 records are naive, and Python refuses to order the two.
  - **Export stages where it lands (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/exporter.py`, `backend/routes/export_import_parts/export_routes.py`)**: the V2 archive is now built in `UPLOAD_TEMP_DIR`, so publishing it is a rename rather than a multi-GB cross-device copy — and that move is off the event loop regardless.
  - **A runtime-omitted export can be imported (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/exporter.py`, `importer.py`)**: `include_runtime=false` left the runtime object *files* out but wrote each manifest verbatim, still declaring every runtime hash — and the importer verified the raw manifests before the plan could strip those refs, so it answered MISSING_OBJECT. The documented "base objects always export, runtime only with include_runtime=True" archive was unusable on every installation including the one that produced it, and the import fixtures never caught it because they pack the union of all manifest hashes regardless of the flag they stamp. The exporter now emits a manifest describing what it actually packed (runtime downgraded to `missing`, root_hash recomputed), and verification runs over `plan.manifests` so archives already produced by earlier builds import too.
  - **Import rolls back to invisible, never to half-published (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/importer.py`, `deletion.py`)**: a failure between `mark_pending_imports` and `clear_pending_imports` used to leave both the markers and the manifests already published on disk, permanently invisible and un-reclaimable with every retry re-failing at the same point, so both handlers now roll back. Rolling back correctly needs two more things. It must clear only the markers whose manifests it actually removed: dropping a marker is what makes a manifest visible, and the callee's store-wide `verify_all_committed()` was *guaranteed* to raise before touching a file on the dominant path into the rollback — `IMPORT_RECONCILE_CORRUPT`, raised by `reconcile()` over that same visible set — so an import the API answered with 422 published its snapshots: listable, loadable, restorable, quota-consuming, with no `ImportBatchRecord` behind them, and unrepairable because recovery declines to act on a FAILED import journal. `_rollback_pending_publish` now takes the `DeleteResult` and clears nothing when the delete raised, and the delete no longer verifies unrelated manifests, since its set is authorized by markers, not by the health of the store. And it must reclaim objects through the shared mark phase: `delete_pending_import_manifests` hand-rolled its reachability set from `verify_all_committed()`, which by construction excludes `audit-*` bundles, the other pending-import manifests and journal-declared hashes — the three root classes `SnapshotGarbageCollector.mark()` protects — so because objects are content-addressed and a pending manifest routinely declares a hash the destination already held, the unlink loop reached through it and destroyed the last reference held by an audit bundle. The unlink loop is gone; the rollback removes the manifest, signature and marker and then calls `self.gc.run()` exactly as `_delete_impl` does, best-effort since it runs inside an exception handler and must not turn a completed rollback into a raised one.
  - **Import bounds its reads before they allocate, and honours its configured limits (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/importer.py`, `object_store.py`, `service.py`, `backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_routes.py`)**: `getmembers()` materialised a TarInfo for every member before `max_member_count` could run, so the limit could not protect the allocation it exists for; the member list is now built lazily and capped as it grows. Every regular member counts toward `max_uncompressed_bytes` (it counted only `objects/`), and the two members read whole into memory — the export metadata and each manifest — get their own caps and bounded reads, instead of being governed only by the 80 GB single-member limit. `import_snapshots` builds the importer with `SnapshotImportLimits.from_config` so the operator's `snapshot_v2_import_*` settings reach the one path they exist for, and two of those settings then needed an actual consumer: `session_quota_bytes` and `free_space_reserve_bytes` were declared and populated but read by nobody, so the only ceilings pass 2 respected were per-archive and successive imports could take a session past the operator's quota with no cumulative bound and drive the shared data volume to zero free space. A single admission check now runs after `build_import_plan` and before pass 2 allocates, projecting only the hashes the store does not already hold — so an idempotent re-import that adds nothing is not rejected — and probing `disk_usage` with the same walk-up `_check_quota` uses; it has to sit after the plan, not in `_check_member_limits`, which sees raw members rather than the sanitized set. `import_v2` likewise streamed the request body into `UPLOAD_TEMP_DIR` comparing `buffer_size` only against 0 and evaluated `max_compressed_bytes` from `stat().st_size`, i.e. after the entire body was already on the volume that carries every session's metadata; the ceiling is now read once before the copy loop and 413 raised the moment it is passed.
  - **Legacy imports release the session id they claimed (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_impl.py`, `import_routes.py`)**: the zip and JSON paths call the same allocator as tar.gz — which takes ownership by creating `sessions/<id>/` — but had none of its cleanup. A truncated archive or a ValidationError left an ownerless directory with no `session.json`: invisible in the UI, never reclaimed, and holding the archive's own id forever, so a corrected re-upload silently landed under a fresh uuid.
  - **A partial `state_sync` no longer blanks the session, and a commit the panel missed still refreshes it (`frontend/src/hooks/api/useWebSocket.ts`, `frontend/src/utils/transformLoadResponse.ts`, `frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`)**: `updateSession` spreads its argument, so a key present with value `undefined` erases the real value — and the restore job's transaction sync carries no `status`, `current_round` or `version`. Every restore progress event blanked the analysis control bar and disabled version-conflict detection. `stopped` was also missing from `VALID_STATUSES`, so a terminated session reloaded as `pending` with an enabled Start button. Snapshot delete now surfaces the backend's 409 instead of an unhandled rejection. The same handler's post-commit refresh was gated on `info.reconnect === true`, a field no backend producer emits — `SnapshotTransactionRecord` has no such key — so the branch was dead, and since that sync deliberately carries no status fields the partial-update block below it wrote nothing either: a restore committing while `SnapshotPanel` was unmounted (reachable, since the page can be reloaded mid-restore and the panel only auto-reopens for a retry transaction) left the client rendering pre-restore state against an already-rewritten server. The dead condition is replaced by a `/session/{id}/load` refetch — idempotent, and it does not tear down a live socket the way a reconnect would — through the same transform path the version-conflict branch uses, deduped per transaction id so the reconnect replay cannot loop it.
  - **The snapshot panel polls, respects its own cap, and can be closed (`frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`, `AnalysisPanel.tsx`)**: the polling effect depended on `startPolling`, whose identity chains to the inline `onRestore` prop, so it tore down on every parent render and its cleanup killed the interval `beginRestore` had just started — the panel's own poller never ran once. Polling is now driven by a state-held transaction id with the handler read through a ref. The parent's retry monitor stops at 150 x 2 s, exactly the backend's 300 s safe-point timeout, and no longer stops silently — but the give-up write defeated that cap: writing `poll_timed_out` through `setSnapshotTransaction` allocates a new object, the selector saw a fresh reference, and the monitor effect, whose dependency array contained the very record its poller rewrites, re-ran; because `stopSnapshotRetryPolling()` had already nulled the ref the re-entry guard could not fire and a fresh `attempts: 0` restarted the loop (measured: 201 requests in 200 ticks). The same structural cause made the auto-open effect level-triggered on a record rewritten every 2 s, so it re-asserted `setShowSnapshotPanel(true)` on every poll and the panel's X was inoperative for the whole life of a retry restore — a `fixed inset-0 z-50` modal returning within a tick of every close, and because of the cap defect for the entire restore rather than a bounded 300 s. Both effects now key off the scalars that actually decide whether they should run, and the open edge is claimed once per transaction; the terminal guard was widened from `committed` to the full terminal set, so a failed or cancelled retry no longer pops the modal back up.
  - **The snapshot UI shows the runtime it has, and marks a corrupt manifest corrupt (`frontend/src/types/snapshotV2.ts`, `components/snapshots/*`, `components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`)**: both the tree chip and the preview block read `manifest.runtime_dependencies`, a key the serialized manifest never contains (it exists only on `SnapshotCaptureRequest`), and the declared TypeScript shape did not match the backend's either — so the blocks were permanently dead and would have crashed if ever populated. Retyped to the authoritative `runtime` manifest; the tree now shows the runtime status, which is what decides whether a restore bounces into the reset/keep dialog. `normalizeSnapshotRecordV2` likewise computes a `SnapshotFailureRecord` for every corrupt V2 entry that nothing read, so a committed manifest failing HMAC/schema/root-hash verification — which `list_snapshots` keeps in the listing on purpose — rendered as an ordinary snapshot: an "Idle" chip, "Invalid Date", "0 files", blank root hash, indistinguishable from a healthy idle one, with Restore offered. The tree now renders the failure code and message in place of the synthesised fields and sorts unusable records last through an `orderKey` helper instead of letting `new Date('')` produce a NaN comparator; the preview returns a dedicated corrupt block instead of reporting a captured time, branch, generation and root hash never read off disk; and Restore is disabled for such a record, since the read-only preflight refuses it with 422 regardless. Delete stays enabled — it is the only way to clear a corrupt entry out of the lineage.
  - **A refused Cancel says so (`frontend/src/components/SnapshotPanel.tsx`)**: `handleCancelTxn` swallowed every rejection into `console.error`, and because the throw lands on the first await the follow-up refresh never ran either, so a refused Cancel produced nothing but a re-enabled button — the same gap `handleDelete` had. Mirrored that path, with the banner at `z-[70]` so it sits above the progress dialog's own overlay, next to the button that produced it.
  - **New tests (`backend/tests/test_snapshot_v2_edge_hardening.py`, `test_snapshot_v2_edge2_{recovery,pre_restore,safepoint,storage,boundary}.py`, `frontend/src/components/__tests__/SnapshotEdge2.test.tsx`, `SnapshotPanel.restore-polling.test.tsx`, `frontend/src/hooks/__tests__/useApi.state-sync-partial.test.ts`)**: 91 backend cases and 12 frontend cases, each verified to fail against the source as it stood before the pass that added it and to pass after. `test_snapshot_v2_gc.py::test_mark_audit_manifests_excluded` is replaced by `test_mark_protects_audit_manifest_objects`; `test_snapshot_v2_import.py::test_import_runtime_omitted_from_archive` now asserts the behaviour its own docstring describes — both pinned the defect — and `test_retire_closes_the_checkpoint_connection_and_evicts` registers a live run task, with a companion case for the no-run leak. Suite: 3467 backend passed / 79 skipped, 229 frontend passed.
  - **Method**: two passes. The first worked outward from the restore transaction; the second re-audited the whole of `snapshot_v2/` plus its workflow, route and frontend surface — deliberately including everything the first pass had just changed — across ten subsystem reviews, and every candidate it produced was then re-verified against the source by an independent reviewer instructed to refute by default. Of that second pass's 30 candidates, nineteen were confirmed as reported and eleven were narrowed (a milder outcome, a tighter trigger, or a different defect at the same site) and fixed as narrowed rather than as claimed; none were fixed on the strength of the original report alone. Six of them were regressions the first pass had introduced or newly exposed, which is why the two are recorded as one version.
  - **Reviewed and deliberately not changed**: the `_restore_resume_node` re-route (`WorkflowState` declares no such channel, so LangGraph drops it before `initialize_round` ever sees it — verified empirically and pinned by a test); `ManifestStore.reconcile()` racing a capture (every caller holds the session lock the capture holds — the dot-prefix filter was added anyway so reconcile is safe on its own terms rather than on its callers'); the un-persisted `upgrade_legacy_snapshot_failure_gate` repair on WebSocket reconnect (cache eviction force-saves, and the upgrade is re-derived on every reconnect); `compute_protected_snapshot_ids`' unfiltered fold of `export_protection` envelopes (recovery now reaps the orphans that made it wrong, and filtering there would open a window where a snapshot an unreaped journal names becomes deletable); `_snapshot_transaction_in_flight`'s inability to distinguish a snapshot transaction from an ordinary route handler holding the same lock (a design change, not a defect — the deferral it causes is now honoured correctly on all three branches); and the unread `poll_timed_out` flag, whose only consumer would be a dialog outside this pass's scope.

- `0.36.11` — The 0.36.10 round trip is verified on the configuration it is named for: Snapshot V2 gate parametrized, continuation checked at the dispatch rather than at the endpoint that only records intent, the two continuation paths no combination reached, frontend refusal handling; version bump (2026-07-29):
  - **The round trip runs under both settings of the Snapshot V2 gate (`backend/tests/test_e2e_http_roundtrip.py`)**: new `snapshot_v2` fixture, applied to every case. The root `conftest.py` sets `SCAI_ENABLE_SNAPSHOT_V2` with `setdefault`, so the whole suite runs on the *enabled* path while production defaults to disabled — meaning the configuration 0.36.10 is named for, the only one on which `/run` and `/resume` call `normalize_disabled_snapshot_state`, was the one configuration its round trip never ran under. The gate is a module global read at import time, so the fixture patches `_config.SNAPSHOT_V2_ENABLED` rather than the environment.
  - **The continuation is checked where it is decided (`backend/routes/websockets.py`, `backend/tests/test_e2e_http_roundtrip.py`)**: the dispatch in `websocket_endpoint` is extracted as `should_resume_workflow` (behaviour unchanged) so tests call the real branch instead of restating its condition. The previous assertions stopped at `POST /run` answering 200 — an endpoint that only records intent and hands back a WebSocket URL — and then read back the status `/run` had itself just written, which says nothing about whether anything would execute. A kernel-less continuation is now checked to reach `initialize_round` with `preserve_round_on_initialize`, i.e. to redo the imported round rather than advance past it and lose it.
  - **Two continuation paths that no combination reached (`backend/tests/test_e2e_http_roundtrip.py`)**: every case continued through `POST /run`, which arms `restore_start_intent` itself and therefore cannot tell whether the import did — deleting `_arm_restore_intent_after_import` left the suite green. Resume on a re-imported runtime archive is where that arming is load-bearing: the id collides, the checkpoint is dropped, the kernel survives, so the runtime reads `complete`, Re-run is not rendered, and Resume — which sets no intent of its own — is the only control. Separately, exporting between Start and the first round completing leaves `current_round` at 0, where both import fixups return early and neither Start nor Re-run renders, so Resume must dispatch a fresh `run()` instead of holding.
  - **The frontend gate is tested on the half only a refusal reaches (`frontend/src/hooks/__tests__/useApi.resume-gate.test.ts`, `frontend/src/components/__tests__/AnalysisPanel.imported-session-controls.test.tsx`)**: the existing helper hard-coded `success: true`, so the `success === false` half of the guard — the only signal three of the four refusals carry — never executed. A `refusedResponse` shape covers it, including that a refusal must not reconnect the WebSocket, since the handler on the other end dispatches the run the backend just declined. A new panel test pins which control an imported session gets in each runtime state: `unavailable` disables Resume and offers Re-run, `complete` offers Resume and renders no Re-run at all.
  - **Still uncovered, recorded rather than implied**: the graph executing for real — every assertion stops at the entry decision, because a step past it needs a live kernel and an LLM — and the round dialog's Continue and Send paths through `blockForwardResume`, which are driven by component state set from WebSocket events.

- `0.36.10` — Imported sessions can actually be continued with Snapshot V2 disabled: `states/` filename rebinding, unusable-checkpoint handling, honest runtime and status marking, recovery-block release, export save durability, json-import session allocation; version bump (2026-07-29):
  - **`states/` members are renamed after the destination session (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_impl.py`)**: new `_rebind_states_member_name`, applied in the `states/` branch of `_resolve_import_dest`. `states/` is the one archive root whose session id lives in the *filename* rather than in a path segment the caller strips — `uploads/`, `workspace/` and `reports/` were already rebound, `states/` was not. An import that had to allocate a fresh id (any re-import of a backup on the same installation) therefore wrote `<archive id>.ipynb` and `<archive id>_session.pkl` into the new session, where nothing ever looks: **the analysis notebook was silently lost on the default export path**, and the kernel pkl sat next to a `_live_pkl` that reported no kernel. The suffix must begin at a `.` or `_` boundary so a hand-built archive declaring a short id cannot rewrite an unrelated file.
  - **An unusable checkpoint is dropped rather than imported (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_impl.py`)**: new `_is_checkpoint_member` (case-folded, covers `-wal`/`-shm`/`-journal`) skips `checkpoints.sqlite` whenever the import allocated a new id. Every row is keyed by `thread_id`, which is the session id of the run that wrote it, and the blobs carry a serialized `AnalysisState` holding the same id, so it cannot be renamed into place. Left behind, the file satisfied the `has_checkpoint` test in `run_analysis` while holding nothing this session could resume from — which withheld the `explicit_start` intent and made Start execute nothing.
  - **A checkpoint only counts together with its kernel (`backend/routes/lifecycle.py`)**: `has_checkpoint` became `has_resumable_checkpoint`, requiring `checkpoints.sqlite` *and* the live pkl. `include_runtime=true` puts the checkpoint in the archive unconditionally while the pkl needs `trust=true` on the way in, and the UI asks those two questions separately — so "checkpoint without kernel" is reachable from ordinary use, and it used to send Re-run into an exact continuation on an empty kernel instead of a fresh round.
  - **The restore intent is armed at import time, not by one button (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_impl.py`)**: new `_arm_restore_intent_after_import` writes `restore_start_intent="explicit_start"` when an already-executed session arrives without a resumable checkpoint. `POST /run` set it, but the frontend only renders that button while `isRerunEnabled()` is true. An import that carried a kernel under a reallocated id has its runtime marked `complete`, so the only reachable control was Resume — which never set the intent — and `resume()` held without executing anything on every attempt.
  - **Runtime availability reads only the live pkl (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_impl.py`)**: `wrote_sandbox_pkl` is now `is_pkl_member and dest_path == _live_pkl(session_id)`. `is_sandbox_pickle` also matches per-snapshot `<snap_id>_session.pkl.zst` backups, so an archive carrying only those made a session with no live kernel claim it had one, bypassing the `is_forward_resume_blocked` gate that exists to prevent exactly that.
  - **A recovery block can be released once Snapshot V2 is off (`backend/services/snapshot_v2/feature_gate.py`)**: `normalize_disabled_snapshot_state` now also clears `recovery_blocked` and `snapshot_transaction.recovery_error`, on a path independent of the snapshot-failure gate so a session carrying only a recovery block keeps its status (the status reconciliation derives from `retry_context.previous_status`, which such a session does not have, and would have downgraded a finished session to paused). The flag is only ever set by journal recovery, which runs only while V2 is enabled, while `assert_recovery_clear` refuses every start, resume, WebSocket attach and kernel launch — so with V2 off nothing could clear it and every snapshot endpoint answers 404. An operator disabling the subsystem to stop the bleeding, or an archive carrying the flag, produced a permanently unstartable session.
  - **Export saves the state it is about to pack (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/export_routes.py`, `backend/routes/sessions.py`)**: the pre-export save and `POST /session/{id}/save` pass `force=True`. `RUNNING` is not in `_FORCE_SAVE_STATUSES`, so the 5-second throttle silently discarded the write that both endpoints perform precisely to capture the latest state; the packer then read a stale `session.json` off disk. A `current_round` still sitting at 0 that way also makes `_mark_runtime_availability_after_import` return before marking anything, producing an archive that claims a kernel it does not carry. `POST /run` and the COMPLETED re-analysis branch of `/resume` force their writes for the same reason.
  - **An imported session does not claim a run nobody owns (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_impl.py`)**: new `_normalize_imported_status` lands `RUNNING` and `GENERATING_REPORT` imports on `PAUSED`. Nothing here owns that run — no workflow object, no kernel, no WebSocket — and the frontend reads the status literally: `running` hides the controls that would start one (including the re-run button a runtime-less import depends on), and `generating_report` matches no branch at all, so the session renders with no controls whatsoever.
  - **Re-analysis of a completed session tells the agent its kernel is empty (`backend/routes/lifecycle.py`)**: the COMPLETED branch of `/resume` sets `runtime_recompute_pending` when it clears an `unavailable` runtime, matching what `/run` already did. Without it `_build_data_context` omits the "KERNEL STATE IS EMPTY" brief and the bioinformatics agent proceeds as though `adata` were still loaded.
  - **The json import path creates a session like the others (`backend/routes/export_import_parts/import_routes.py`)**: it now goes through `_allocate_import_session_id` with the same remapping and rebinding as tar.gz and zip. It was the only path that took the id straight from the uploaded document and then stamped the caller's `user_id` onto it, so posting `{"id": "<another user's session>"}` handed that session over.
